ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e190	The police have always had the right to use hidden coercive measures to carry out their social mission, and all the governments in power have been involved in contributing to this. In order for the police to be able to adapt to a crime scene changing, we politicians sometimes need to consider what tools the police will have to use to investigate and combat crime. Although hidden coercive agents are legal methods, their use has often been a controversial issue. No one wants a monitoring society, and in our open and free democracy, privacy and legal certainty must be maintained at all times. Therefore, the threats to society and the way we face them must always be weighed and measured against fundamental democratic values. There are in particular two important driving forces behind the government's proposals that we are now going to debate in the Grand Duchy. First of all, we see that the police are faced with new and difficult tasks when the crime scene changes. Even if total crime goes down, we see a clear increase in the most demanding and organized crime. The global threat picture is becoming increasingly complex and unwieldy. Globalisation brings the world closer together, and for Norway and Norwegian interests, developments and events will quickly have serious consequences, including at home. The development of more cross-border crime calls for police’s ability to adapt. Conflicts, natural disasters and poverty are driving many refugees. Migrant flows have increased sharply in the last year, including in Norway. People in a vulnerable situation are at risk of being exploited by organised criminals and in the ultimate consequence become victims of trafficking in human beings. Increased immigration can also increase the risk of various forms of crime, including hate crime, labour market crime and drug crime. The number of foreign fighters who have travelled from Norway has doubled. In 2009, when the Methodological Control Committee presented its statement, the PST had knowledge of a handful of people who traveled to join extreme Islamic groups abroad. Today, the number of suspected foreign warriors is only about 90 to Syria and Iraq. Norway has become a clearer part of the enemy image among extreme Islamists. The PST considers it possible that terror will be attempted during the current year. In addition, the threat from right-hand extremes is considered to be increasing. The second driving force behind the matter we are now dealing with is technological development. The use of advanced encrypted communications is available to everyone. In many cases, encryption is done automatically, for example: by increasing the number of Internet clocks, such as Facebook, Google and Twitter, that encrypt all information flows to, from and between their users. Last week, the NRK introduced the same thing at home. This is good. Technology has given us innovative solutions, which safely promote value creation, trade and new social forms. But at the same time, new technologies are helping to make crime more cross-border. Criminals are oriented towards those countries that have the largest digital values. Norwegian businesses are increasingly exposed to online crime, and there are large numbers of them. PST warns that in recent years many intelligence organisations have been very active in obtaining information from Norwegian computer systems. Encryption makes much of the information that the police have legal access to, inaccessible. It gives the police considerably less opportunity to capture and prevent serious crime than they had just a few years ago. That increased use of encryption creates challenges for police and intelligence, is confirmed by the Lysne Committee, which was set up by the government, in the Digital Vulnerability Study — Secure Society. This backstop makes it necessary to strengthen the instruments that protect and defend Norwegian citizens and Norwegian interests. The government’s proposal on hidden coercive measures is based on a balance where the objective of effective crime prevention must be balanced against the interests of privacy and legal certainty. Privacy is an important democratic value, of course. At the same time, we must defend democracy against the forces that will destroy it. Privacy is generally protected by both the Constitution and the European Convention on Human Rights, the EMC. The condition for the intervention of the authorities in this respect is that the purpose of the intervention falls within the exceptions set out in Article 8.2 of the EMC. National security, public security and crime prevention are the exceptions that allow for privacy intervention. Furthermore, it is required that the intervention is governed by law. Finally, intervention must be deemed necessary in a democratic society. The proposition details each of these conditions. I would also like to make it clear that several of the Committee on Methodological Control's proposals have been rejected by the government, bearing in mind that they are too intrusive. The proposals in the present proposal deal with a certain extension of the use of today's coercive agents. For example, communication control is also open to cases involving gross human trafficking, trafficking in human beings, detention and child abuse. In addition, a new coercive agent is introduced, namely data reading. It is important to clarify that the use of coercive measures is to be done on suspicion of the most serious crime. Legal certainty is ensured by judicial review of the use of coercive substances, in addition to never being used if not necessary for investigation or prevention. General principles of proportionality and proportionality always apply when there is a question of using a coercive agent. In addition, we have control functions through the Communications Control Committee and the EOS Committee, and the former is strongly strengthened. The Committee asks the government, in its examination of this proposal, to ensure that the Communications Control Committee lists the number of cases in which coercive agents are being used, the suspicions of the person and the hidden coercive substances that have been used. The methods proposed would have to involve obtaining a greater amount of information than at present. The Committee therefore points out the need for a review of the rules for the use, storage and deletion of surplus information by the government. The proposal to introduce data reading as a hidden coercive agent is probably the most controversial in the proposition. I am pleased that the proposal as such is widely supported here in Stortinget. This tool makes it possible, in the most serious cases, for the police and the PST to reveal information that would otherwise render encryption inaccessible. Nor shall the use of data-reading by police happen to most people, as some try to create the impression. The police shall only monitor the individuals suspected of having committed or going to commit serious integrity violations, such as killing, child abuse and terror. I would like to add that I am satisfied that the government did not consider banning encryption. Other countries have considered going to this step, which, in my view, would be counterproductive. Data reading is an intrusive method and it is difficult to find the correct threshold for when this is permitted. The majority of the Judicial Committee, after an assessment, has chosen to raise this threshold somewhat in relation to the proposal. This means that data reading cannot be used to detect simple drug abuse, simply drug-healing and negligent drug-healing. With this proposal, the government strengthens the fight against the most serious crime, such as terror, espionage and proliferation of weapons of mass destruction. Through the processing of the Storting, privacy has been further enhanced. I thank you for your cooperation with the Committee. I'm assuming that the respective parties are presenting their minority proposals.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e230	The Labour Party believes that the police must have a tool that keeps up with the crime scene and the technological developments we have in the country today. It's about having to have the opportunity to do their social work in a good way, even at a time when society is as digitized as it is now. This development will be even faster in the future. That is why we support the need for the police to have access to new methods. At the same time, we believe that this bill from the Council of State has been a very difficult matter. The reason is that several of the methods are highly invasive. Some of them have had a low threshold for when they can be used, and can therefore be used in cases that are difficult to view as particularly serious. It is also demanding when the proposition does not highlight the consequences of the overall sum of methods now available to the police, for example. the importance it has for surplus information or for the source protection of journalists. This is part of what we have been promoting autonomous proposals on the matter. The proposal for surplus information has also been supported by the majority of the committee, and we are pleased with that. The Workers' Party has been in contact with police representatives in order to know as much about their challenges as possible. We have also met with stakeholders with insight into technology and its challenges and met critics of increased use of hidden methods to assess their objections. For us this has been absolutely necessary in order to be able to conclude in a good way in what has been a difficult matter. We believe that the legislative proposal is too weak in several areas, and more issues should be clarified, especially with regard to the new and necessary method of data reading and the practical and fundamental questions it raises. The proposal refers to Sweden and those who stopped the submission of a data-reading proposal, and it is shown that they will re-promote this. But there's nothing about why this was stopped, or what they're going to do differently in the future. The Swedish proposal was stopped because the Swedish Data Protection Authority considered it contrary to national law and the European Convention. On 16 May this year, the Swedish government launched a public investigation into data reading. This shall be delivered in the autumn of 2017. Where they order a comprehensive investigation on all aspects of the implementation of data readout as a police method: the extent to which there is, the extent to which the total number of methods is, how to process surplus information, the cost of use, technology, software, to mention something. In short, everything that should have been captured and illuminated in this proposition. In this case, the Workers' Party promotes a number of independent proposals. The main lines of our conclusions are that it was necessary to raise the threshold for the use of data reading from simple drug offences, simply to heal and negligently to heal serious drug offences and to heal badly. Otherwise, the use of methods would become disproportionate to the criminal act. We have received support for this from the majority of the committee, and that is a cooperation we have appreciated. We would also prefer that the majority resonate differently around the police law § 17 d, which concerns PST’s access to the use of preventative hidden methods. The majority provide uncritical access to the use of data reading, which is a rather invasive tool. Data reading as a preventative hidden method is very invasive, and it has the potential to challenge the Constitution. They rely on quotations from the Riksadvokat. However, the quotations do not refer to the police law § 17 d or prevention methods, they refer to the criminal procedural law § 222 d, the access of the police to use hidden methods to avert a criminal act and how to understand the term ‘dismissing’. The Workers' Party believes that preventive data reading under the authority of the Police Security Service is a method that should only be used in the most serious cases, such as preventing terrorist acts and terrorist associations, while the Right and Advance Party, together with the Christian People's Party, will allow the prevention of threats to authorities. We can see that from the reference from the police law § 17 d to the criminal proceedings law § 216 o on data reading and the criminal law § 263. We promote an independent proposal on this limitation and hope that as many parties as possible will give their support to this and to this understanding of the seriousness of the Constitution § 102. Governments have proposed to increase the resources of the control committee so that the legal framework for data reading and other hidden methods can be superseded. We think that's good. But we should also like to see that there were procedures for controlling the software during data reading. We therefore propose the development of supervisory procedures for this, including for uninstallation. In a number of other areas too, we believe that the legislative proposal goes too far. So that's going to be equal to-- let's say that's going to be equal to-- let's say that's going to be equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say that's equal to-- let's say-- let's see. access to the use of communications control to locate communication facilities, i.e. the ability to track people under concrete suspicion, beyond the current provisions on personal technical tracing – which have a much higher threshold for use. In our opinion, the government has not argued satisfactorily for this in its proposal. They also provide an opportunity to track people to a far greater extent than at present by means of false base stations, without this being limited to their geographical location, the time period for suspected residence or associated with specific criminal offences. Instead, the police have been given a fairly broad access to use this method. We are promoting our own proposals in both of these areas, in order to define and make responsible. This bill is sometimes characterized by urgent work. We saw this particularly clearly in the question of hidden camera surveillance according to Section 202(a) of the Criminal Procedures Act. In the fifth paragraph, the second sentence says that camera surveillance can be both with and without the possibility of recording audio and image material. This would change Norwegian law in this field dramatically. Access to hidden monitoring of sound can be obtained through completely different paragraphs, such as: the provision for room listening, which has a higher threshold. When the Council of State was asked about this in the spontaneous question hour in May, he defended it. When he got the question again, in writing, he went back on it. We are pleased that the majority on Stortinget now joins the Workers' Party to remove the word "sound" from the provision on hidden camera surveillance. Finally, I would like to say that hidden methods are most necessary for the police. It's about detecting, averting and sometimes preventing serious criminal acts. It supports the Workers' Party. At the same time, these methods require good balances for legal certainty, privacy and democracy — isolated to the individual approach, but also to the sum of the methodology used. In a number of areas, the government's proposals are short. That is the background for our proposals in this matter. Then it only remains for me to address the suggestions we are included in the setting.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e309	Let me first thank the case rapporteur and the Committee for Good Cooperation – because it has been a demanding matter. The issue we are dealing with today raises major questions of principle – about the relationship between the state and the citizen, about legal certainty, the rule of law, freedom of expression and privacy. Our primary task is to set the framework for a safe society, a society where children and young people can grow up without being afraid of being exposed to anything criminal. Unfortunately, we know that this is not the case today. But a lot goes well in our country. Crime has gone down, which is very good, but although there are fewer cases, the police have not gotten less to do — rather the opposite. Crime has become more complex, cross-border and, in particular, more serious. We therefore have a responsibility to assess and update the measures taken by the police, so that they have the opportunity to do the job we have put them to do. Hidden coercive agents involve interference with the privacy of individuals. Therefore, a requirement for the use of these instruments by the police requires thorough assessments, with more considerations to be weighed against each other. The changes we are adopting today are not a release of instruments for the police. It's very close to some mass surveillance or free from the police to monitor them. This is targeted interception and control in cases that deal with very serious crime, and it requires judicial control and also good control of its use in retrospect. These are short and well-needed changes to ensure police tools to combat serious crime. Abuse of children online is so cruel and grotesque that we have difficulty imagining that it is possible. According to Kripos, there has been a sharp increase in such cases lately. More and more children are subjected to blackmail and are manipulated to engage in online sexual activity, and because the police do not have the tools they need, the result is that offenders go free. It's unsustainable. I am therefore pleased that the changes we are adopting today will strengthen the police's important fight against criminals. But if we are to combat violence and abuse against children, it does not help to extend police powers – this must be combated in several areas. I am therefore very pleased that a unanimous decision was taken last year by the proposal of the Christian People's Party on a comprehensive escalation plan to combat violence in close relations, including in particular violence and abuse against children. This plan shall ensure health, justice and family measures. It is essential to combat this horrific crime. We as society must ensure that children and young people grow up in a safe environment, and then we must also do what we can to prevent, detect and combat child abuse. For the past few weeks, the newspaper has written many reports about the ‘shadow children’ — children who are under assault and forced labour — who live in slavery. Because slavery is not over, and it must be combated in several areas. Trafficking in human beings is very serious and serious crime and is difficult for the police to detect and investigate. I am therefore very pleased that today we are adopting the amendment of the law, which is a follow-up to an earlier representative proposal we had, to allow the use of communication control in human trafficking. This is an important step in the right direction, so that the risk of backers gets higher, more can be taken and more victims get their freedom. These are just a few examples of important issues for which today’s decision provides important guidance. It has also been claimed that we adopt rules that allow us to have a thought policy – that is wrong. Restrictions on the use of hidden coercive agents do not change, even if we are expanding by a new method, namely data reading. There must still be very strict conditions under which the police can make use of it and, moreover, there must be no change in the requirements for conviction. With this new method, we will help the police to access communications that they previously had access to, but are now encrypted. That's why it's good that the police now get some of the lead criminals have got. At the same time, we know that data reading will not lead to the police getting all the communication they want and that they are initially allowed to obtain. Therefore, this measure will not solve all problems – on the contrary, good old-fashioned police methods are still needed to combat crime. It is important that data reading is reserved for the most serious crime. Where we are going to set the threshold for data reading is a difficult balance between privacy considerations and society's need to combat criminal acts. It is therefore difficult to find the actual point of intersection of the method. But the Justice Committee makes important adjustments on the basis of what the government proposed. We're raising the threshold when data reading can be used. This means that when the legislative changes we are adopting today come into force, data reading cannot be used to detect simple drug abuse, simply drug healing and negligent drug healing. We believe that this is an important clarification, which we are pleased that the Committee has agreed on. The Christian People's Party is concerned that the use of communication control should be closely followed. Therefore, we are pleased that the evaluation of the EOS Committee shows that this is working and that it enhances the use of hidden coercive substances. In the future, we will have to assess the strengthening of the EOS Committee's budget to ensure the optimum functioning of the work. It is also important that the annual reports of the EOS Committee and the Communications Control Committee show that communication control is not abused by the police or the PST — nor is urgency misused. It is important that this work continues, so that we know that the law is being followed, and that the tools are being used as intended. Therefore, it is also important that the capacity of the Communications Control Committee be strengthened so that the sample can handle its function in the future. The contribution to the sample is proposed by the government to be almost tenfold, from 0.7 million. NOK 6 million. kr. Of course, we support that. In our view, when there is such a change and an increase in budgets, it is not appropriate to evaluate the Communications Control Committee now, as the Workers' Party and the Centre propose. Therefore, we would rather promote a proposal for evaluation of the sample three to five years after the entry into force of the legislative changes related to this proposition. Then it will be much more appropriate to do just that. The debate on this legislative proposal has also raised doubts as to whether an extension of access to sound and video recordings has been proposed. Everyone agrees that there should not be such an extension, but the definition of camera surveillance in the new Section 202 a fifth paragraph may appear somewhat ambiguous as it appears to include both sound and image material. Therefore, there will also be an overall committee in favour of the removal of «sound» from the definition of camera monitoring in the fifth paragraph of Section 202a. That's good. Specific proposals have also been made covering the software to be used for data reading. I believe this is an important issue. That is why I am pleased that this should be regulated by regulation. This regulatory work must address the issue of ownership, ensure that – at least as far as possible – Norwegian ownership of the software, that there are supervisory procedures for the security of the software, that data transmissions related to monitoring are kept as far as possible within Norwegian jurisdiction, and that procedures for uninstalling applications that are no longer in use are developed. All of this is important to avoid abuse. Legal certainty for individuals must be ensured. It's absolutely crucial in a state of law. But as the police lawyers pointed out during the hearing in the Justice Committee, there is no legal certainty that the police have no means to combat crime. The individual victim must also be guaranteed the right to privacy and privacy. That is why the Christian People's Party believes that there are important changes we are adopting today.

Why use this algorithm if you want to claim that this is not a private home break-in? Then one just makes it harder for itself. The entire Committee points out that the proposal suggests that the police should be able to physically break in order to place and remove equipment in connection with data reading. I ask the Minister of Justice to clarify this issue during the debate. Initially, we in the Center Party were willing to expand the possibilities that the police have for making society safer, but an important commitment for us was that important care should be taken. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e598	Let it be said that today the police also have access to hidden coercive measures, for example. telephone interception and camera surveillance. The assumption is, of course, the approval of the Court of Justice, and so it should continue. It is the court that decides on the fight against crime and balances it against other overriding societal considerations, in particular privacy, and lays down conditions for its implementation. Most of the coercive measures currently used by PST were adopted by the Stortinget in 2005. Since then, both the threat and the technology have changed considerably. The use of hidden methods by PST is currently giving us less access to information than was foreseen by the Stortinget in 2005. The proposal currently under consideration by the Stortinget expresses the need for a comprehensive update of which coercive measures the PST should have access to in order to combat terrorism and serious crime. In recent years, the PST has described a serious threat picture, with increased terrorist risk and increased serious crime across borders. Unfortunately, we have seen a blessing. Several of our allies are haunted by gross, cruel terror, where human dignity has been brutally meddled down. The increased use of encryption creates challenges for police and intelligence, is evident in the Swedish Commission’s investigation, Digital Vulnerability — Secure Society. It does not help to intercept between a and b when the communication is between a and a. Or in another way: today you can access the computer of others and retrieve information without communicating with each other. There is no doubt that encryption has brought the police on the defensive in the fight against serious crime. Data reading will enable the police to access information in a computer system at such an early stage that encryption cannot make information available. Data reading as a method will fill the intelligence gap created today by encryption. Data reading will be a targeted attack on the serious crime taking place in the digital space and weakening social security. There is a need for the safety of the population to weigh more heavily. Hidden use of methods is invasive and must be used with caution. Therefore, data reading will only be opened in exceptional cases. The assumption is that there are grounds for suspecting serious crime which could lead to a prison sentence of up to 10 years or more, for example: Terrorism, trafficking in human beings, kidnapping and intelligence against state secrets, i.e. the type of crime that can hit the individual and the state hardest.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e646	Police in Norway have always had access to hidden coercive measures to investigate, prevent and avert serious crime. The government is now choosing to introduce a new coercive tool, called data readout. This means that the police can read information that is available on a PC or a mobile phone by breaking into it. It's almost like researching a room or a dwelling, just that the search is digital. The police have long asked for this method, since criminals communicate through digital and encrypted services that police do not have access to. The WhatsApp app is an example of an encrypted service that the police are unable to access today, although there is reason to suspect terrorist planning or a major drug introduction. In other words, police coercive measures must follow technological developments and be in line with the method employed by criminals. Today's threat level, seen in the light of events in particular: Paris and Brussels, unfortunately, are also one of the reasons why the police must be as well equipped as possible to carry out their social mission. I record that some representatives have repeatedly made a point that the police are now monitoring your mobile phone or your computer. No, any coercion in the Criminal Procedures Act is targeted at individuals suspected of having committed a serious criminal offence, such as killing, possession of images and terrorist planning. It is therefore not your mobile phone that is of interest to the police. The same thing will also apply to data reading. Telling the police to monitor your computer will therefore not be correct. It's not some kind of mass surveillance. With the approval of the court, the police shall only be able to monitor the computer of a potential offender, terrorist or drug baron. For communication control, this concerns about 100 cases per year, and not 5 million, which correspond to the population of Norway. I do not understand that it is presented as a threat to privacy that the Norwegian police, under strict legal control, are given access to information on persons who are suspected of a criminal offence for dubious reasons. Police today are often on the sidelines, given an out-of-date piece of legislation, and the criminals go on with their actions. We see that the amount of surplus information will increase in the introduction of new coercive measures. Therefore, the Stortinget has also asked the government to review today’s legislation on the use and processing of surplus information in order to strengthen privacy. The alternative to the government's proposal is not to do anything. At least I'm not sure if I'm comfortable with it in the light of today's threat level.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e826	First, I would like to thank the proposers for addressing an extremely important topic and important proposals – on which the Committee has been involved on several occasions during this period. It's done a lot and adopted a lot. Much work has also been done on the specific issue of whether professional secrecy stands in the way of detecting violence and abuse. Proposals are based on the NOVA report ‘Demand obligations, information rights and information obligations. Regulations and practices’.» It was drafted on behalf of four ministries in March 2013. The report shows that confidentiality rules are generally perceived as valuable and necessary. A central response from the field is that they are not perceived as an obstacle to cooperation in practice. However, the report also shows that the informants do little to read the laws, circulars and guides, despite the fact that they feel uncertain about the rules in force. It is throughout that management’s knowledge and commitment to the legislation is of great importance to how well the individual employee is aware of the legislation. Another prominent feature of the report is that the exceptions to the obligation of professional secrecy in particular are not known. The Committee assumes that the findings described in the report are followed up in the work of the government to ensure the proper functioning of the obligation of professional secrecy and will not constitute an obstacle to the detection of violence and abuse. The Committee is also committed to ensuring good cooperation between the various departments, between health services, child protection, police, etc., and to having good regulatory competence to detect violence and abuse. The Committee is committed to, and refers to, the work undertaken to promote the obligatory and comprehensive plan of escalation aimed at reducing violence and abuse against children, which is to come to the harvest. The Committee has great expectations for that work. The Committee is also subject to Section 196 of the Penal Code on the so-called elimination obligation. In the opinion of the Committee, this is important, because for many children, it is that someone intervenes from the outside, perhaps the only rescue they have. The well-known recognition of the elimination obligation is also important for the Committee. The Committee emphasises that we are pleased that in its prioritisation directive, the Advocate General has warned that police and prosecution authorities need to focus more on enforcing this provision. In this context, a unanimous committee refers to the letter of reply of the Council of States, which refers to the fact that a work is being done and that he will consider whether the abolition obligation should be extended to more offences. As he writes: ‘I will consider these issues in more detail with a view to drawing up a possible proposal for an extension of the countermeasure’.» The Committee is concerned that children should be heard and that it is important that the professional bodies try to care for the needs of children to be protected from the importance and necessity of passing on to their representatives. In this context, we refer to studies that underline that children need to tell their side of the case without this being affected by parents' right of access. To this end, a unanimous committee also promotes proposals aimed at the assessment by the government of restrictions on, or suspension of, the right of access to the file and the right of access by the parties to documents relating to child protection when it is best for the child. There are many joint committee comments and some specific comments, but there are also nine proposals from the Labour Party and the Centre. I don't have time to explain these proposals, I expect them to do that. But on behalf of the Christian People's Party, and perhaps also on behalf of the majority, our assessment has been that in the case of example. The decommissioning obligation, which we are extremely concerned about, is doing very much good work. There are many investigations to be completed over and over again, and we have great expectations that it will be followed up – not only in the escalation plan, but also in individual cases that will come later.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e850	Violence and child abuse have serious and long-term consequences for individuals. There is a serious social problem, and there is a need for broad cooperation between different public bodies such as health professionals, child protection, police, teachers — bodies working on the basis of different laws, different guidelines, and belonging to different cultures. The case has arisen because of the need for action to prevent professional secrecy and regulation from standing in the way of a good offer to children exposed to violence and abuse. There is evidence of the need to take action to ensure that the practice of legislation in the various levels of legislation is based on correct legal understanding and takes care of the best interests of the child. In Report 3/13 on professional secrecy, disclosure rights and disclosure obligations, the Norwegian Institute for Research on Growth, Welfare and Ageing, NOVA, has a good example of how demanding it really is to practice, for example: the provision on exemptions from the obligation of professional secrecy. The report also shows that the conditions of professional secrecy are not well known. It is a challenge that the rules on professional secrecy, disclosure and information rights are contained in different laws. They are partly different in design, including the Administrative Act, the Health Personnel Act, the Special Health Service Act, the Child Protection Act, the Children's Hospital Act and the Training Act. It's a challenge to coordinate. The NOVA report also shows that legislation is poorly harmonised, in particular with regard to the possibility of sharing confidentiality-related information with health professionals and administrative staff. The obligation to provide information does not always take precedence over the obligation of professional secrecy in matters relating to the protection of children. In order to ensure the child’s best in violent and criminal proceedings, the Workers’ Party considers that there is a need to see the different laws in the context and to have a comprehensive review of the legal provisions, so that we collectively regulate professional secrecy, disclosure and information rights. There must be no formal obstacles or lack of knowledge that make it difficult for the public to identify and follow-up cases of violence and abuse against children. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e1029	First, as the rapporteur-general, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in this matter. The majority of the Committee, composed of the representatives of the Labour Party, the Right, the Progressive Party and the Christian People's Party, are committed to the very purpose of the Schengen Agreement, namely to make it easier and more effective for persons to travel in the Schengen area. In addition, the Norwegian export industry benefits greatly from simpler and faster cross-border transport. The majority agree that border cooperation and more open borders increase the risk of cross-border crime and illegal immigration. The Schengen Agreement therefore contains a number of compensatory measures to combat both cross-border crime and illegal immigration. This includes, inter alia: the rules on the requirements for external border controls. In addition, the establishment of close operational police cooperation and judicial cooperation on visa issues will take place. Schengen cooperation has been under pressure in connection with the increased flow of migration. The absence of a national border control requires increased and enhanced control at the external borders of Schengen. The border control at Schengen – Europe’s – is not good enough today. In this context, the majority of the Committee understands that, in line with EU countries, the government has applied the provisions of the Schengen acquis to reintroduce temporary checks at internal borders. The EU is therefore actively working to design different measures to ensure that Member States with external challenges can manage them. The majority of the Committee calls on the government in the negotiations with the European countries to work for satisfactory external border control and underlines the importance of effective control at the external borders in order to prevent uncontrolled secondary movements in Europe. If Norway were to withdraw from Schengen cooperation, which the Committee of course does not recommend, the costs of border control will increase. In that case, Norway will lose access to the Schengen Information System, which is an important source of information exchange with the corresponding authorities of other Schengen States. The police's ability to detect, prevent and prevent organised crime will be significantly reduced, and our security could be weakened. The majority emphasise that the part of the Schengen Agreement governing police cooperation is essential for national security. Furthermore, the reintroduction of border controls would result in significant competitive disadvantages for Norwegian industry and the economy, which the Committee would not recommend.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e1044	The purpose of the Schengen Agreement is to make it easier and more effective for persons travelling in and between Member States. The Schengen Agreement was already in place in 1985 and has since had several updates and extensions. The Schengen Agreement is a pattern of the Nordic passport union and has established extensive police cooperation in this region. Norway and Iceland, as members of the Nordic passport union, were associated with the 1996 Schengen Agreement, as passport freedom had already been established with Denmark, Finland and Sweden. Today, we see that the Nordic passport freedom is under pressure and that the temporary ID and passport controls introduced in Denmark and Sweden threaten the Schengen Agreement, which has been in force for more than 20 years and today includes 26 countries. The agreement abolishes border controls on persons and goods between countries and opens up extensive police cooperation. Europe's two passport-free areas, Schengen and the Nordic passport union, are linked, but there is still a need for passports for countries such as the United States and Japan. United Kingdom, Ireland and Switzerland. For traffic in and out of the Schengen area, there is passport control at both entry and exit, what we refer to as the external border of Schengen. The practical consequences of the Schengen Agreement are therefore the most important for people to avoid passport controls, and not least the fact that the Norwegian export industry, which is currently under great pressure, has great benefits from facilitating and speeding up the transport of its products to the European market. Of course, the majority of the Committee is aware that open borders increase the risk of cross-border crime and illegal immigration. It is also easier for asylum seekers with a residence permit in one Schengen country to travel to another, and similarly that the authorities of one country have the opportunity to reject asylum seekers who have been refused in another Schengen country. The agreement also provides for common rules on combating terrorism, illicit drug trafficking and other forms of serious international crime. Among other things, police patrols will be given the opportunity to persecute people across the border on a fresh track and to conduct observation of suspects. However, such activities are subject to national control and foreign police officers cannot exercise police authority on foreign territory, for example. arrest. For the Workers' Party and the majority of the Committee, it is important to clarify that we record that border controls at the external border of Europe are not sufficient and that the government, in line with other EU countries, must apply the provisions of the Schengen acquis to reintroduce temporary checks on persons at the internal border. The Labour Party will also stress that the Agreement contains several compensatory measures to combat cross-border crime and illegal immigration. The absence of national border control requires control at the external border of Schengen. It is therefore essential for the EU to put in place measures and common legislation that contribute to strengthening the security of borders, while at the same time providing mechanisms to ensure rapid and effective border crossings that do not weaken the competitiveness of Norwegian businesses and the economy. The Workers' Party cannot support proposals that would weaken our security and contribute to the reduction of police's ability to detect, prevent and prevent organised crime. The Schengen Information System and the Visa Information System are important sources of information exchange with the corresponding authorities of other Schengen countries. In conclusion, I would like to stress from the Labour Party and the majority that the part of the Schengen Agreement that regulates police cooperation is of great importance to our national security, both now and in the future.

This isn't something we in the Center Party have come up with all of a sudden. I would like to stress that we have a number of limited agreements on cooperation between Norway and the EU which, irrespective of our connection to Schengen, are to be continued. Examples of this are cooperation agreements with Europol and Eurojust. Such agreements can still be extended in spite of Norway’s withdrawal from border cooperation, in the same way that the United Kingdom and Ireland have judicial policy agreements with the EU without being themselves members of Schengen. It is about being solution-oriented, and it is possible to find solutions in cooperation with countries and the EU. Our draft representative points out that a reestablished Nordic passport union could be a better solution than the border cooperation we have under the Schengen Agreement today. Nordic cooperation is important for Norway. Norway was part of a Nordic passport union for several decades before we joined the Schengen Agreement, but we see that for different reasons it is not necessarily possible to find a Nordic solution in the short term. The work for this must still begin now. I would also like to stress that modern technology does not require border control to be particularly invasive, time consuming or to the detriment of those who cross borders. The security check may take longer than a passport check. There is therefore no significant argument against reintroducing national border control. I would like to reiterate that the objective of introducing border controls for travellers from Schengen countries is to ensure the security of the kingdom, to prevent cross-border crime and to gain better control of who comes and lives in Norway. Therefore, the country must withdraw from the parts of the Schengen Agreement which deal with border control. Finally, I would like to address the proposal of the Centre Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e1421	Today, the paper deals with the 2015 Annual Report from the Norwegian National Human Rights Institute, which I would like to mention in part as NIM further in my post. The new national human rights institution was created by law of 1 July 2015. The institution is organisationally placed under the Storting, but is otherwise independent. Paragraph 1 of the National Institution Act requires NIM to ‘promote and protect human rights in line with the Basic Law, the Human Rights Act and other legislation, international treaties and international law’. NIM thus has an important function in our democratic society. According to Section 11 of the Act, NIM shall send an annual notice to the Stortinget on its activities and the development of the human rights situation in Norway. That is the message we are currently processing. The institution has recently been created, so the annual report is not long. I would like to point out that the message is rich anyway. It gives a good picture of the tasks the institution will work with. NIM shall elucidate human rights issues in Norway, operate holistic surveillance of all human rights and provide advice to authorities and others. There is a wide range of issues the institution will issue and provide professional advice and input on. These include international obligations in the field of asylum and refugee law, drug care, ethnic discrimination, the situation of indigenous peoples, national minorities, psychiatric treatment practices, religious freedom and trafficking in human beings. A natural part of being a child involves testing out your own and the boundaries of others. Some children’s exceedances require correction and, where appropriate, treatment. The main measure is to prevent — develop and deploy good measures early, and build and disseminate skills to prevent this from happening. Around one-tenth of the children will continue to behave in this way. 20-30 pst. of all abuses against children and young people by other minor children, and about half of those who commit adult assault, committed their first assault in childhood or as a teenager. They may be traumatised themselves, have other and more concomitant mental illnesses or have a developmental disability. But we also know that treatment helps. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e1606	Children and young people who commit abuse are a sensitive and difficult topic that we rarely discuss. The screening will be launched on Friday, but I will nevertheless refer to some findings that I have already learned, since this matter is being dealt with here today. The survey shows that treatment provision for young people with disabilities is available in all four health regions, but there are few specialised services. However, many of the treatment units feel that they are not given the treatment they want to receive to this group of children and young people and, above all, call for more competence. NKVTS recommends, inter alia: increasing the competence of the BUP in the treatment of young offenders. Furthermore, they recommend the assessment of units with special competencies anchored in the specialist health service of all regional health establishments. The need to build on the competence of young people in other sectors, such as care, school and justice, is also emphasised. This shows that we have a way to go. This applies in both ordinary and emergency positions. However, if there should be mispositions, Bufetat has procedures to deal with this. Furthermore, the minority calls on the government to ensure its own institutions for children and young people who commit sexual abuse. Many argue that children’s homes can play an important role both in the questioning situation and in the follow-up of young offenders. Several children’s homes already have competence in and experience of interrogation and follow-up of this group of young offenders. However, there is a need to further explore the role of children’s homes in the case of young offenders, and not least what resource needs will be required if children’s homes are to be assigned new tasks in this field. Today’s capacity situation at most children’s homes is already demanding, as the Committee is aware. In conclusion, I would like to thank the representatives who have raised this issue about young attackers. Early help and treatment are essential to prevent new abuses. I will now, together with the Minister of Health, follow up the mapping we have received from the NKVTS, and come back to Stortinget in an appropriate manner with a follow-up to this.

The majority of the Committee considers that it is natural to carry out a community reform and regional reform at the same time and that the new regions enter into force on 1 January 2022. By 1 December of this year, the county councils will have to decide who they may want to join, and in that process the municipalities should also take part. The majority adopts a new regional structure, with a background in local processes, in spring 2017. In the new regionally elected level, the role of society is strengthened. The regions will retain most of the tasks they already have today and will be given new ones. The Committee considers that regional planning is a central instrument for the exercise of the societal development role and that these plans must be based on the activities of regional bodies, on municipal planning and on regional planning and on state planning and activities. This will also lead to better coordination and more effective planning. The majority clarifies the need for the responsibilities and obligations of regional government bodies to participate in the planning processes. Tasks that facilitate the use of political judgement shall, as a general rule, be transferred to the regionally elected level. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e2175	Societal development, which is still characterised by conflicting objectives, demands greater overallity and coordination in many areas: knowledge, economic development, land-use planning, transport, public health and culture, resource management and, above all, climate and environment. The Green Party therefore joins a unanimous committee to support a regional reform that facilitates social development based on regional advantages and assumptions and strengthens the ability of regions to meet new tasks of better coordination. However, climate and environmental issues are no longer sectoral interests equated with many other interests. They are the essence and driving force of the fundamental change project that many call the green shift and that society as a whole must implement. This has too little impact on the debate on regional reform. In 2016, when we are embarking on a major regional reform, we need to have a clear idea of the objective. The aim must be to make the regions central tools of the green shift and to prepare the regions to go through this shift – and to bloom afterwards. There are areas where it's decent to get bigger. We need a stronger regional level under elected government that can take regional leadership in cooperation with municipalities, businesses and government. The regions must be clearly defined in terms of responsibilities and powers for the tasks that are important for regional development and for the coordination of these tasks with the activities of the municipalities and the State in the region. When we discuss larger regions, we also need to ask some key questions: Will the space for action and authority for sustainable social planning be increased? How do we implement the major measures for green food development, public health, culture, nature and food soil? How do we create the best possible development without losing values? How do we cut emissions and get ahead of ourselves in achieving the objectives of the Paris Agreement? The Green Party believes that it must be an objective and a clear prerequisite for the creation of new regions that they become effective low-emission regions with strong economic development by 2030 in the context of strict cultural, natural and environmental requirements. Overall, this is the basis for the overall objective: regions with a high quality of life. Much of the debate is about the need for new and important tasks for regions. For the Green Party, it is essential and democratic that all decisions are taken at the lowest decision-making level, as close as possible to the population – that is, often in the municipalities. At the same time, however, decisions that have regional or national implications must be governed by a regional or nationally elected level. That is why we support proposals from the Labour Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Centre that tasks that facilitate the use of political discretion are added to the popular regional level, and services such as Innovation Norway, secondary education, dentistry, collective services, etc. are maintained at the regionally elected level, while administrative tasks currently assigned to the State Environmental Protection Department and the Agricultural Department must be maintained. Whereas it is necessary to strengthen the regional planning authority, the institution of objection and the possibility of using regional planning provisions; Better coordinated spatial and transport planning at regional level is good both for municipal and for national levels. The counties have to take more control over their own future, and the counties have made clear what tasks they want. Now it's up to the government to clarify what they're emphasising in a merger process. The importance of the societal development role that the states already have today is under-communicated and underestimated. One of the prerequisites for the success of regional reform is that this role becomes clearer. Any new and larger regions must be given a clear mandate and be responsible for implementing changes. Countries need to be given tasks and powers that strengthen the ability to drive sustainable social development. Climate change and environmentally sound regional development are key to future welfare. It is a prerequisite for competitive business, and public services and well-being must, in themselves, not undermine the life base of a secure future. The Paris Agreement, which has been signed by the government, makes much greater demands for social change in Norway than has so far been incorporated into Norwegian policy. The regional reform should be the first stage in which this premise is built up from the ground up.

It is former TMO that, in the context of the conference, changed the name of the Trust Elections Scheme – from the Trusts Council. They do so in the context of the introduction of a general duty of protection. I think it's a nice and sensible gesture that's a modernisation of the name. Long processing times in the clearance cases have been challenging both for the defence and for the individual, and in 2014, as the Committee is well-known, there was a particularly demanding situation due to errors and shortcomings in the introduction of the new Mimir procedure system. The Ombudsman’s subject and the Committee were rightly very critical of this development in the last year’s report. I shared this concern, and we also immediately put in action. I therefore informed the Stortinget of what measures were taken to reduce the length of proceedings to an acceptable level. Last year, the length of proceedings at the Defence Security Department and the National Safety Authority increased by more than 30 pst. Mimir was repaired, and procedures and procedures were changed so that the clearing process became more efficient. The last time we discussed this in the committee was in connection with the EOS Committee's annual report, and I therefore thought it was a good opportunity to give an update on the current state of play. Today, the procedure period is significantly reduced both by the Defence Security Department and by the National Safety Authority. The last report showed a high level of target achievement. For example, the Defence Security Department residue list has now been reduced by 65 pst. from the highest measurement last year. On 25 May this year it was approx. 2 600 ongoing cases against 4,464 in September 2015. 2 600 is considered a relatively low number because there will always be some residues in such a large system when they treat approx. 17 000 clearances per year. The national safety authority has reduced the average duration of proceedings in complaints by more than 60 pst, from 285 days on average in previous reporting to 117 days in the first tertiary 2016. In the case of the NSM clearance cases, the average duration of proceedings in the previous reporting period was 133.6 days, with the first tertiary being 82.9 days. I am well pleased that development continues to go the right way, but it is a work we are still going to follow closely from the department. I would also like to inform you that the future objective of the defence is to clarify the clearing issue before you meet for first-time service, and continue. The system was tested last year at the session center in Kjevik with good results. The aim is to establish equivalent procedures at all session centres. I am pleased that both the committee and the committee are following this work with interest. When I stood here a year ago, it was a historic year. On 1 January 2015, an important social reform was launched. The protection of the country's interests, values and territory now applies to all Norwegian citizens. There is now a common right and a common obligation to implement the protection obligation. 2016 is also a brand year, because this summer the first year school meets women under protection for the first time. But when that's said, I always remember, in all the congregations, that Norway has had women in service for decades. That means that a lot is already well organized so that there are also women who are inside and serving, but it needs to be done even more, because we can expect, if not a sudden rise, then at least an increase in the number of women who are both sheltered and also serving in Defense. I am pleased that the Ombudsman reports that the introduction of the general protection obligation has been well received. This is also reflected in the fact that a number of measures are being taken to address some of the main challenges. The work on equipment and accommodation adaptation is well under way. We've had women who have served in Defense for a long time, and in recent years a lot has been done to facilitate a more balanced gender balance. In conjunction with the Committee's comments on last year's Document 5, I note that the Committee is pleased that the Defence Service has taken the general duty of protection seriously. Extensive and targeted communication has been carried out against both guardians and parents. Furthermore, there has been a focus on equal treatment between women and men in the session process, and the proportion of women among guardians is also expected to increase from the summer. Of course, personal clothing and equipment must be adapted to both sexes, and the defence has therefore been working on improvements in the selection, quality and availability of equipment, and also assessed adjustments to framework agreements where necessary. Since 2011, there has been an increased commitment to improving living conditions for the soldiers. The standard of older building stock remains a challenge in some places. Therefore, the Defense has prioritised garrisons that need the most upgrade. A project is under way at Madla Camp, and plans have been made for the next few years at Ørland Main Aviation Station and in Huseby Camp. At the same time, the defence will continuously identify and assess the necessary needs related to lay-offs, sanitary conditions and other facilities. Both female and male soldiers should feel welcome and respected when they meet at their service. It affects recruitment, motivation and mastering. That is why work on further developing a leadership and soldier culture that is characterized by mutual respect, responsibility and cohesion is so important. I am therefore also very pleased that the Ombudsman points to the good attitudes they take when they are out and inspect different departments. The defense does very much good work in this area, and the work continues with full strength. Overall, the defence must be well prepared for such a comprehensive reform. I have clear expectations that the defense will facilitate well, even if not everything is in place right away. I also assume that the defense makes local adjustments and provides temporary solutions where there is a need for them. But the big picture is still very positive. We're in a situation where we can choose between the best fittest and most motivated from a year's coal. This year’s safety investigation shows that the defence is a very good impression of the need for protection throughout the year. The well-being is very high, and the environment is good. More than eight out of ten of those asked state that they thrive quite or very well. The study also shows that very many would choose the initial service if it had been voluntary. It's good to see that so many people want to contribute to something bigger than themselves.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3056	After this long introduction, I will repeat what this case is about, the law on the conversion of the state enterprise Aerospace Industrial Maintenance Norway into a limited liability company and the decision on the change in state ownership. Let me first of all thank the Committee for good cooperation. It took us a little while to figure out that we really had the exact same goal. We were a little bit suspicious of each other before we could sit down and see what we wanted with AIM, and what the goal was long-term. So it's rare I come with a joyous outburst from this pulpit, I have to admit that, but this is actually a joy that the Storting should accept with great enthusiasm. We need more of these things. This could potentially mean dozens of new, technologically advanced jobs that could potentially be added to Rygge in Eastfold. It can't be that there are too many jobs in Eastfold, there are at least some who may need some jobs in Eastfold, and now they can get there. So it's a victory for this government, and not just negative about what's going on with the airline fees and other things. In concrete terms, the decision of the government to convert AIM from State enterprises to public limited liability companies is a matter of concern. It is a wise decision which, strangely enough, the Workers' Party, the Christian People's Party and the Center Party are against. Fortunately, both the Labour Party and the Christian People's Party join the solution subsidiaryly. The public limited liability company AIM with a State holding of 50 p.m. will ensure that future maintenance of F-35 engines could be added to Norway. AIM will also be able to compete for maintenance of other countries’ F-35 engines. The transformation into a limited liability company is therefore forward-looking. The company entering the AIM owner’s page will necessarily need to invest in equipment that enables AIM to deliver competitive offers. Then we're talking about hundreds of millions of dollars, e.g. an engine bench, which is very advanced equipment. Such investments will be significant and there is no reason for this to be financed by taxpayers from the State budget. Private capital provides room for more flexible and commercial activities. It is a good example of public-private cooperation in practice, where there is private and public capital, while private companies come in with new solutions and competitiveness and make it possible to compete outside. That's something the state can't do, or it's done it before, but it hasn't been very successful. On 3 June, it was notified that AIM Norway purchased the Belgium Engine Center, an engine centre for the repair and maintenance of the F-100 engine, which is located in the fighter aircraft F-15 and F-16. This company is also well positioned to become the European engine centre for F-35. BEC is owned by Pratt & Whitney, one of the world's leading engine manufacturers. The purchase implies that AIM takes a large part of today’s market for the maintenance of fighter aircraft engines in 14 different countries. The company will then be in good position for future agreements. The conversion into a limited liability company takes over all the assets, rights and liabilities of the entity by the State entity AIM Norway, while the State acquires all the shares in the company. The decision Stortinget takes today, will allow the state to reduce its ownership to 50 pst. The State may also sell entirely out of the company, but it assumes that the purchaser already holds a State holding of at least 50 pst. A lot of people will think of this as a fixed invitation. It's not against me. In any case, any transaction will be made after negotiation. Maintenance of fighter aircraft engines may not sound very sexy, and many will perhaps accept the news with a large gasket. They shouldn't. The potential in the maintenance of fighter aircraft engines is significant. We're potentially talking about hundreds of jobs, further development of technological skills, and not least awareness that acquisitions have not only a cost side, which we tend to look at, but also a significant income side. In fact, the Norwegian defence industry exports 99 cents for every penny we spend on acquiring defence equipment. It is our responsibility to pass on this message that the defence industry is of great importance to Norway, important to jobs, important to competence, important to technology, important to districts, important to subcontractors, and important to Norway abroad. We have to take care of the defence industry. It must not be sold, but be kept.

In the end, it is very important for us to point out that new owners will provide new rounds of reorganisation and uncertainty, and that employees should be guaranteed their rights and pension plans in the State enterprise when it is transformed into a limited liability company. It also applies to the contract for a closed, professional pension scheme in the State pension fund for employees of the company before the transformation into a state enterprise. As I said, I am pleased that we in the Committee have very clear comments on these points.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3100	As the rapporteur has thoroughly explained, this case concerns transforming the state-owned company Aerospace Industrial Maintenance Norway SF, also known as AIM Norway, into a limited liability company and empowering the government to change its state ownership of the new limited liability company. AIM Norway is currently a state-owned company operating maintenance tasks related to central air defence equipment such as F-16 fighter aircraft and Sea King rescue helicopters. The purpose of the transformation into a limited liability company is to further safeguard the company’s position in an international competitive situation, to enable the company to have more owners and to be associated with strategic partners who can participate in and further develop the company, with the investment needs required when the new armaments phase takes place, such as the new F-35 fighter aircraft and the new helicopters. It will provide greater flexibility to adapt the ownership structure to what is best for the company in the long term. It is an objective to ensure that the high-tech competence of the company remains in Norway today, and that a government sale will provide the Norwegian defence industry with a good opportunity to strengthen its position and expand its operations at AIM Norway AS. Furthermore, it is important to ensure operational continuity – that the operation is continued in its entirety without prejudice to customers and co-contractors. Existing agreements with the Defence are transferred to the new company. The competence of the vis-à-vis owner of the board is not affected by the transformation, nor will it have financial or administrative implications for the State. Employer's rights and obligations and employees' rights and pension schemes are continued. The availability and maintenance of highly qualified personnel in Norwegian hands is essential to ensure adequate preparedness and the operational capability of the Defence Agency. In the right, it is important to ensure that the head office of the new limited liability company is located in Norway, and I would like to stress that it is important for the Right to ensure good framework conditions so that we can maintain and further develop a competitive Norwegian defence industry. The appointment of the Committee ensures a good balance and facilitates the strengthening of private ownership, while taking into account that this is a sensitive and strategically important business area for Norway, is ensured by establishing a lower share of ownership of 50 p.m., while opening up full sales if the purchaser has a minimum State holding of 50 p.m.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3252	Several have not asked for the floor for case No. 12 and 13.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3266	This matter concerns a very important issue, namely to facilitate the conditions for combating threats to the independence and security of the country and other vital national security interests, as well as ensuring legal certainty for the individual and to ensure trust and simplification of the basis for the control of preventive security services. Today, we are facing a complex risk and threat picture. The security policy situation is constantly changing. Society is becoming more and more vulnerable, and technology is evolving rapidly. Therefore, it is important that the Safety Act keeps up with this development. The fact that this is taking up many shows the many hearings that came to work on the revision of the law. We can also see that the proposed changes to the law take care of most of the recordings made in the consultation round. There is full agreement in the committee on this matter, and that is good. I would like to thank the Committee for good and constructive cooperation. I just want to go through some main points quickly. Changes are now being made to the general scope of the law, in addition to the extension of the law to all acquisitions into critical infrastructure. In this context, I would like to point out that the petroleum and energy sectors are also covered by the Safety Act. Furthermore, exceptions to the duty of security clearance and authorisation are now being made to the members of the Government and judges of the Supreme Court. This has been a long-standing practice, and there is no reason why there should be a distinction between these positions and those already exempted, such as: us deposit representatives. An alert obligation is also introduced for establishments governed by the Security Act which acquire knowledge of a planned or ongoing activity which may pose a non-negligible risk to the independence and security of the State. The national response function for serious data attacks against critical infrastructure, NorCERT, is now included in the law. The digital infrastructure warning system and the processing of personal data are also included in the law, and a specificisation of the tasks will be included in a regulation. The current Information Security Regulation, which provides for the retention of safety-related incidents for at least five years, is now incorporated into the law. As far as the clearing authority is concerned, there has long been a need to establish a new clearing authority structure with few but competent clearing authorities. Today we have 42 clearing houses. It creates challenges. Professional environments are fragmented, and there's a biased distribution of issues. In addition, increasing competence is being required to deal satisfactorily with clearance issues. Fewer clearing authorities will obviously increase legal certainty for individuals and provide a better basis for non-discrimination. The proposal proposes to reduce these from current 42 to 7 and that there should be only one civil sector clearing authority and one defence sector. However, it is clear from the statutory audit that the three EOS services continue to clear up their own staff, as well as that, for constitutional reasons, the courts and the ministry of the Prime Minister continue to be maintained as their own clearing authorities. It is also new that in the case of acquisitions of critical infrastructure, the obligation to provide warning is not conditional on the acquisition being defined as a security-graded acquisition; furthermore, businesses are also required to notify authorities if they wish to implement acquisitions into critical infrastructure which may pose a non-negligible risk to the independence and security of the empire. It is important to note that an obligation is now also introduced for establishments subject to the Security Act to notify the Superior Ministry of a knowledge of a planned or ongoing activity which may pose a non-negligible risk to the establishment or implementation of security-threatening activities. It is, of course, essential that authorities be given the opportunity to react before a security-threatening situation becomes a reality. It is therefore important to provide a basis for the King of State Council to intervene in acquisitions of critical infrastructure where such a risk exists. Overall, this is a means of preventing espionage and sabotage, which will lead to control by the operators themselves, assessing risks and applying risk mitigation measures before any warning is sent to the authorities.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3294	The rapporteur has presented the excellent report for a unanimous vote. I only have a moment that didn't come into the notes, and as I would like the Council of State to remember, when you go from 42 to fewer, it is very important to ensure sufficient procedural capacity, so that there are no bottlenecks. Now new things can happen, and then it's important that you keep it in mind from the start. This also applies to capacity following complaints, it has happened that they have also accumulated.
