ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3301	I am very pleased to note that the Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee has given a unanimous vote with full support to the proposal for amendments to the Security Act. I would also like to thank the President-in-Office for both a clean and good presentation and for the work he has done. This is the first part of the work on the Safety Act. It's a safety committee working on a comprehensive law audit, but the government was of the opinion that it was important to deal with some of the more urgent challenges quickly, and so we made these changes in the Security Act now. Although there is talk of what could be called minor changes to the applicable safety laws, the proposed changes are important enough. As the Joint Committee itself points out, today we are facing a very complex risk and threat picture. The security policy situation is changing steadily, society is becoming more vulnerable, and technology is evolving at a very rapid pace. Therefore, it is important that the Safety Act keeps pace with developments. The legislative proposal is an important measure to strengthen our preventive safety. With regard to the proposal for a new clearing house structure, the main aim is to improve the quality of the clearing house. This is, as we have discussed in the matter of the Ombudsman’s annual report, very important decisions, especially for the persons concerned, but also for the businesses that depend on the security clearance of their personnel. A reduction from 42 units to mainly two central authorities, one in the defence sector and one in the civil sector, will contribute to the enhancement of skills and quality and equal treatment in personnel clearance cases. It is also both an improvement in efficiency and a debureaucratisation. The point of the representative Hansen is very good. I will not rule out that there may be any accumulation of balances right in the transition phase, but what is also in the proposition, in paragraph 12.2.2, is just an explanation of how to transfer resources internally from the authorities and sectors that have had their own clearing authorities to the central clearing authorities that are now becoming. For example, when you have: in a department or in a sector shall no longer be responsible for clearing, resources shall also be transferred. It is the Ministry of Justice that is responsible for the new civil sector clearing authority, so there will be a framework transfer to the Ministry of Justice from other departments in the civil sector – and similarly in the military sector, where the Defence Security Department will be provided with resources from the departments in the sector that will no longer be clearing authorities. With regard to the proposal for the processing of personal data, it is important to clarify that this access shall only apply in cases where it is necessary for the national safety authority to carry out the important task of dealing with serious data attacks against critical infrastructure. Surplus information, i.e. information that goes beyond the purpose of the acquisition, will neither be retained nor provided to others. The protection of privacy has been safeguarded by the fact that a proportionality assessment of the need has been carried out, against legitimate data protection interests. The basic requirements of the Data Protection Act shall apply to the processing of personal data. Here it's about the lawmaking of a practice that already exists, and I think it's an independent point to get this kind of practice into the form of law. Lawmaking is a clarification of the framework to be applied and therefore means strengthening privacy. Furthermore, I would like to stress the importance of the proposals for the two new provisions on warning and authority for the King of State Council to take decisions to avoid the occurrence of security-threatening situations. One provision requires the owners of critical infrastructure to have an obligation to assess the risk of espionage and sabotage in the acquisition of such infrastructure and to alert the authorities if the risk is assessed as not negligible. The government shall ensure that the establishments concerned are provided with clarity as to the overall authority to which it is to be notified. The second provision requires establishments currently subject to the Security Act to notify if the establishment becomes aware of a planned or ongoing activity which may involve a non-negligible safety risk. Both new legislative proposals can be described as a safety valve or an emergency brake and are therefore not often used, but the proposals are, in my view, good examples of preventive thinking and not only reactive to prevent the occurrence of security threats, such as: an increased possibility of espionage or sabotage on socially important objects or installations. It is important that the authorities are provided with the necessary tools for intervention where independence and security of the country so require.

Repurchase agreements are of the utmost importance for the Norwegian defence industry and ensure market access to our partners and are an important basis for larger deliveries to the foreign market.

In fact, I don't think so many people are aware of how important this industry is to Norway. I would therefore like to draw up a few examples of what the repurchase and industrial cooperation initiated by the authorities have meant. Kongsberg, in cooperation with Raytheon in the United States, has developed the NASAMS air protection system for the defence needs of the last half of the 1980s and the first half of the 1990s. The system has been delivered to six nations, including the United States, and several nations are planning acquisitions where NASAMS is concerned. Continuous further development and upgrade in cooperation with the Defence and Export Customers make Kongsberg today deliver one of the world's most advanced and food-efficient air protection systems in its kind. So far, NASAMS has generated more than 13 billion. kr in export income. Cooperation with Raytheon was established as a result of the industrial cooperation agreement concluded in connection with the acquisition of missiles for our F-16 aircraft in 1995. The cooperation agreement initially had a duration of 10 years. In 2005 and thereafter in 2015, the agreement was renewed for a further 10 years. So far, one industrial cooperation agreement from 1995 has provided the basis for 30 years of strategic cooperation and can be further extended, as Raytheon has now become Kongsberg's partner for the marketing and sale of both NSM and JSM in the United States. Another example: Nammo at Raufoss is the sole supplier of the engine for the air-to-air missile AMRAAM, the main weapon of both the US and Norwegian air defence. AMRAAM is used on F-16 and in NASAMS. Long-term commitment to the development of the production of rocket engines on Raufoss is the basis for success. At the end of the 1990s, Nammo and the Norwegian authorities focused on a rocket engine technology programme, among others. on the basis that Norway had decided to develop the Naval Strike Missile, NSM, for the new frigates and the coastal corvettes, and to participate in the development programme of the ESSM Mission Sea Sparrow, which is used for the air protection of the frigates, and the missile that is the short-range weapon in our F-16 aircraft. This is the starting point for Nammo to have one of the world's most advanced competence environments for the development and production of rocket engines. Success with AMRAAM has opened the doors to become the supplier of several missile programmes to both the United States and Europe, among others. to the United States missile AIM-9X and the British LMM missile. In the last ten years, Nammo has delivered rocket engines worth about 4 billion. kr. My main point is, as I hope everyone will register, and I think most people understand that industrial cooperation agreements are not a plan economy that has been misunderstood. The defence industry is important for defence, for industry, for value creation and for Norway. That is why we must bet on both industrial cooperation agreements and – I was saying in the worst case – buy-backs, but industrial cooperation agreements are the most important thing.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3566	I would also like to thank the rapporteur for a good piece of work here, and I fully agree with the comments that have been made by the members of the Committee relating to the Los Projekt, which have been going on since 2001, with the latest specifications for requirements from 2008. That is why I do not spend much more time on it or on the individual investment projects, because they seem to me to be well covered by both the rapporteur and others. So instead, I want to spend some time saying something about how we and Defence Building work for redemption and noise action on properties in Ørland. In general, it is important to stress that for us it is very important to fulfil the commitments to society, which are defined through the regulatory plan and other legislation. So it's notable in the case of Ørland that the regulation does not provide a basis for expropriation. This means that both redemption and noise measures are voluntary schemes for the individual owner and are concluded in accordance with normal contractual principles. In the process, we place great emphasis on non-discrimination between those affected, whether in terms of redemption or noise measures. The island community will be treated within the same legislation and practice as other local communities affected by large state construction projects. Defence construction is based on the principles of the Law on Remuneration in the event of property ownership or, as it is called in good Norwegian, the Law on Remuneration in the event of voluntary purchase of buildings for noise-free use. On average, defence buildings have paid more than 40 pst. over taxed market value of the properties in which the owner has occupied the property. Farming in Ørland is also important. In cooperation with the agricultural authorities, we want the areas around the air station to continue to be characterised by active agriculture. In those cases where the farm owner chooses to have the house redeemed, the operation for most can largely continue as before. As this is done by means of regular contractual purchases, the owner is also free to accept an offer. Defence buildings have estimated that 175 dwellings in the red noise zone will be offered redemption. Included in this figure are 34 agricultural properties. Of these 34 farms, 26 are active farms. The rest are left out. As I said, for buildings with noise-free purposes, we base the principles of the remuneration law. As of 1 June 2016, Defence Building purchased 42 dwellings divided into 37 landowners, 27 of whom purchased a new dwelling. 15 owners of land have purchased a new dwelling within the purchase price of Defence Building, and one owner of which has bought a dwelling outside Fosenhalvøya. Three have purchased land and are in the process of building a new dwelling. Seven have paid more than the purchase price for the new dwelling, and one has purchased a dwelling outside Fosenhaløya. The reason they have paid more than the redemption sum is that they have purchased a new dwelling with significantly higher benefits, such as: rental unit, completely newly built dwelling or a significantly larger area. Of the detached dwellings, three secondary dwellings and one of the dead have been redeemed so far. In addition to covering these costs, an average of 40 pst. over taxed market value, Defence Buildings has covered the costs of adaptation and relocation with a round sum of NOK 120000 and the document fee for the purchase of new dwellings. Additional expected costs have also been covered. Just because this follows common contractual principles, legal and technical assistance expenditure has also been covered by Defence Buildings. I would also like to remind you – and I think that is an important point in this context, because there are many housing owners around Ørland who are uncertain about what they want to do – that it has been suggested that housing owners can wait until 31 December 2019 to decide whether to want redemption or noise measures. This means that in practice you get approx. two years after the new aircraft have arrived to assess what is the noise picture before deciding whether to take action or noise measures. In my opinion, the overall picture of the measures and the way we work is good and raw. I think it's something that serves both the defence and the civil society in a good way.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3622	The Government has negotiated an agreement between the EEA/EFTA States and the EU on a new period with the EEA Financial Mechanism and an agreement on the Norwegian Financial Mechanism, both for the period 2014-2021, as well as an additional Protocol on trade in fish between Norway and the EU. This is the fourth agreement on the contribution of EFTA countries to the social and economic development of the less developed countries of the EEA. A broad majority of the Committee agrees with the government that it is in Norway's interest to contribute to this, and that these contributions also contribute to the promotion of bilateral relations. Just to facilitate a knowledge-based debate, today from the Centre Party, we would like to make a suggestion. Here we are asked by the government to prepare a study showing the correlation between changes in the exported quantity of all relevant varieties of Norwegian fish and changes in the customs rates of these species, from 1990 until today. When the representative Hansen, formerly a leader of the Europa pipeline, thinks that I am de minimising customs duties on Norwegian fish, I expect him to support this proposal. ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3758	I would like to start by thanking the Committee for having shown great goodwill and prioritized to dealing with this matter before the end of the session this spring. The EEA funds are, as many have mentioned, Norway’s solidarity contribution to social and economic development in Europe. EEA funds contribute to growth and development in Europe, which is Norway’s main export market. The funds shall also contribute to strengthening bilateral relations between Norway and the beneficiary countries. It is therefore designed to facilitate, inter alia: Norwegian enterprises, researchers, municipalities, social partners and non-governmental organisations may participate in cooperation projects with EU countries. The new agreements with the EU on new EEA financial instruments mean that the funding period has been extended from five to seven years, i.e. 2014 to 2021. The EU’s exit requirements were 84 p.m. increase, while the final result was about 11 pst. increase in the annual contributions. Inflation in the EU during the five-year period before the negotiations took place was total about 10.7 p.m. This implies that there was only a marginal real increase in contributions compared to the previous period. As a central part of the follow-up to an active European policy, the government has very ambitious objectives for a more targeted use of EEA funds in the years to come. We will increase efforts to improve Europe's competitiveness and create a forward-looking internal market. The aim is to stimulate innovation, growth and jobs through innovation, research and education. At the same time, we should use EEA funds to strengthen Europe's energy security and contribute to offensive climate policies. We will also increase support for police and judicial cooperation between Norway and the recipient countries, including asylum and migration cooperation. All European countries have a common responsibility here. Furthermore, we also want to strengthen minority participation in politics and social life and protect their rights. Parts of the support will be earmarked to improve the living conditions of space people. At the same time, we continue to support civil society and a private fund to promote cooperation between social partners. This also includes measures to combat cross-border labour-life crime. Let me also now take the opportunity to highlight some good examples from the current period, from a few of my trips to the beneficiary countries. In Estonia, we have had great success with the Green Industrial Innovation programme. It has brought together professional environments across borders and created new and smart solutions in areas such as energy monitoring, welfare technologies and environmental technologies. It is particularly gratifying that these projects have led to cooperation on their own, even after the end of the support period. When I recently visited Tallinn University of Technology, I also learned more about a scientific cooperation project with the University of Bergen, funded by the bilateral research programme. The research concerns communication between nerve cells relevant to diseases such as Alzheimer’s, Huntington’s disease, autism and depression. This is a very good example of the use of EEA funds for applied research in a bilateral context. Another important area of activity, both in the previous period and in the new period, is the inclusion of vulnerable groups. Education is the way out of poverty for many space people in Europe. Early school leaving increases in the first row the possibility of children completing primary school, but also for more people taking longer-term higher education. Bulgaria is a country with a large population of space. However, it is a challenge that children from space families are not liquid in Bulgarian when they start school. This is due, inter alia, to: that there is little childcare provision and that space people live and live in isolation from the Bulgarian-speaking majority in the country. For this reason, it is important that children receive a preschool offer with language training, so that they are better equipped when the school course starts seriously. We have used EEA funds in Bulgaria for, inter alia: Preschools for rum children in Sofia. This shows that the EEA funds are used on vulnerable groups and benefit some of the weakest in Europe. We are now going into a hectic period of negotiations with 15 recipient countries about the device on the funds. I want to keep Stortinget informed about progress and results. The agreements on new EEA funds are a concrete follow-up to the government’s work programme for cooperation with the EU. I am convinced that we have already achieved a great deal with the EEA funds and that, with the approval of the Stortinget today, everything is at the heart of helping us to create a green, competitive and inclusive Europe with the new EEA funds.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-06-08.ana.ud2369e3821	There can be no doubt that the solution we have obtained in the negotiated agreement on improved market access to the EU for processed products is very important for the Norwegian fishing industry and for the fishing industry. With regard to the comments and proposals for a study of the Centre Party, I would like to remind you that the Government has recently presented the Maritime Food Industry Communication, which makes it clear that access to key inputs such as raw materials, labour and capital, as well as market access – with two lines under market access – is central to the competitiveness of the maritime food and maritime food industries. The fact that there has been an increase in exports over the past 25 years cannot be taken as proof that EU customs duties are not effective. Seafood industry itself has shown in several consultations here on the Storting, at the latest in the context of the globalisation notification and the maritime food industry notification, that customs duties on the EU market are very negative and that their removal could provide a basis for increased value creation in Norway. Obviously, when you meet with 13 pst. customs duties on e.g. smoked salmon is not particularly high in its potential to smoke salmon in Norway and to be able to sell it when the Polish processing industry imports the salmon as a raw material and processes it there. I simply note that the centre party is on a radical collision course with what is a requirement of an overall industry, both from the fisheries and industry sides, that this with better market access and lower customs duties must continue to be a priority work for the government. It will continue to be vis-à-vis the EU and in other contexts globally, where there will be negotiations on improved market access. We have a fantastic fishing industry in Norway, we have a fantastic fishing industry, not least we have an enormously exciting breeding industry, but the potential for which we can get out of these industries is closely linked to market access and what opportunities we have to be competitive in the markets. And I would like to stress that even low rates of duty can be of great importance in being competitive in a industry where the margins are often very small.
